<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### The continuum existence problem

A phase transition in a continuum particle system can be expressed as a loss of regularity of its thermodynamic free energy. We consider an existence problem: can a single species of particles in $\mathbb R^3$, interacting through a stable radial pair potential, have a temperature singularity at one fixed density, and can the same critical inverse temperature work throughout an open interval of densities? We use the following precise class of potentials and normalization.

A potential $\Phi(x)=\phi(|x|)$ is *admissible* if it is Borel measurable, $\Phi(0)=+\infty$, and $\phi$ is finite and locally bounded on $(0,\infty)$, with the following properties:

1.  $\phi(r)\to+\infty$ as $r\downarrow0$;

2.  there are $0<r_1<r_2$ and $a>0$ such that $\phi(r)\le-a$ for every $r\in[r_1,r_2]$;

3.  $\int_R^\infty r^2|\phi(r)|\,\mathrm dr<\infty$ for some finite $R$;

4.  there is $B<\infty$ such that, for every finite configuration, <a id="eq:stability-target"></a>$$\label{eq:stability-target}
     U_N^\Phi(x_1,\ldots,x_N):=\sum_{1\le i<j\le N}\Phi(x_i-x_j)\ge-BN.$$

<a id="eq:free-energy-definition"></a>
Let $\Lambda_L=[-L/2,L/2]^3$. With free boundary conditions, define <a id="eq:canonical-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:canonical-definition}
 Z_{\Lambda,N}^{\Phi}(\beta)=\frac1{N!}
 \int_{\Lambda^N}e^{-\beta U_N^\Phi(x_1,\ldots,x_N)}
 \,\mathrm dx_1\cdots\,\mathrm dx_N,\qquad \beta>0,$$ where $Z_{\Lambda,0}^{\Phi}=1$ and $e^{-\beta\infty}=0$. The position measure is Lebesgue measure, independent of $\beta$; no temperature-dependent normalization is included. For a density $\rho>0$, the canonical free-energy density is $$\label{eq:free-energy-definition}
 f_\Phi(\beta,\rho)=-\frac1\beta
 \lim_{L\to\infty}L^{-3}\log
 Z_{\Lambda_L,\lfloor\rho L^3\rfloor}^{\Phi}(\beta),$$ when the limit exists. The problem is to find an admissible $\Phi$ and a fixed $\rho$ for which this limit is finite on an interval of positive inverse temperatures and fails to be $C^1$ across an interior point.

Write $D_\beta^-$ and $D_\beta^+$ for the left and right derivatives with respect to inverse temperature.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exist an admissible radial difference-only pair potential $\Phi(x)=\phi(|x|)$ on $\mathbb R^3$, a nonempty open interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$, and one $\beta_c\in(1/2,3/2)$ with the following properties. The potential satisfies $\phi(r)=o(r^{-3})$ as $r\to\infty$. The free-cube limit <a href="#eq:free-energy-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-energy-definition">[eq:free-energy-definition]</a> exists and is finite for every $\beta>0$ and every density $\rho>0$. For every $\rho\in I$, the finite one-sided derivatives at the same critical point satisfy $$D_\beta^- f_\Phi(\beta_c,\rho)
 >D_\beta^+ f_\Phi(\beta_c,\rho).$$ Thus one potential and one critical inverse temperature give a strict canonical temperature singularity throughout $I$.*

</div>

The construction uses a narrow low-energy shell inside a repulsive core, together with a summable family of radial attractions at successively larger ranges. Thus the theorem concerns an engineered potential in the explicit integrable-tail class above. It does not assert a transition for the ordinary $12$–$6$ Lennard–Jones potential, and it makes no identification of the coexisting states as conventional liquid, vapor, or solid phases.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and the role of the fixed density

The continuum phase-transition existence question belongs to the program highlighted by Simon’s problems in mathematical physics (Simon 1984) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Simon1984](#ref-Simon1984)]. Here the tail hypothesis is exactly condition (iii). The construction also gives $\phi(r)=o(r^{-3})$, but does not establish a bound $|\phi(r)|\le C r^{-3-\varepsilon}$ with any fixed $\varepsilon>0$. Accordingly, the theorem does not claim to resolve formulations of the historical problem that require such a power-law margin.

Earlier rigorous continuum transitions include Ruelle’s result for the two-species Widom–Rowlinson model (Ruelle 1971) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Ruelle1971](#ref-Ruelle1971)]. Israel’s convex construction also applies to hard-core continuum particles: a spherically symmetric pair perturbation and a chemical potential produce two invariant equilibrium states of different densities (Israel 1975, sec. 6, Theorem 8(a)) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Israel1975](#ref-Israel1975)]. The hard core in that result excludes a positive interval of separations; the potential constructed here is finite at every positive separation. Lebowitz, Mazel, and Presutti later proved a transition for a model with attractive pair and repulsive four-body interactions (Joel L. Lebowitz, Mazel, and Presutti 1998, 1999) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LebowitzMazelPresutti1998](#ref-LebowitzMazelPresutti1998), [LebowitzMazelPresutti1999](#ref-LebowitzMazelPresutti1999)]. The four-body term remains part of that model.

Recent constructions impose other structures on the interaction. He, Jauslin, Lebowitz, and Peled prove distinct-density Gibbs coexistence for suitable parameters and sufficiently large range in a model with a box-modified Kac pair interaction (He et al. 2026, Equation (1.13) and Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[HeJauslinLebowitzPeled2025](#ref-HeJauslinLebowitzPeled2025)]. It depends on a fixed partition into cubes, and the reference interaction acts only within each cube. Dereudre and Renaud-Chan prove low-temperature coexistence for saturated finite-range interactions satisfying a Peierls condition (Dereudre and Renaud-Chan 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DereudreRenaudChan2026](#ref-DereudreRenaudChan2026)]. Their radial-kernel example integrates over unions of particle neighborhoods, giving a many-body energy rather than an ordinary pair sum (Dereudre and Renaud-Chan 2026, sec. 2.3, Example 3, Equation (2.18)) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DereudreRenaudChan2026](#ref-DereudreRenaudChan2026)]. Our final interaction is a radial function of the particle difference, with all cross-cube interactions retained. Moreover, density coexistence alone does not imply a temperature corner at a fixed density. We obtain two supporting slopes with the same density coordinate and different energy coordinates, the information needed for the strict derivative jump in <a href="#eq:free-energy-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-energy-definition">[eq:free-energy-definition]</a>.

The long-range comparison is a version of the Lebowitz–Penrose mean-field limit (J. L. Lebowitz and Penrose 1966) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[LebowitzPenrose1966](#ref-LebowitzPenrose1966)]; its extension to superstable interactions is classical (Gerardi et al. 1973) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GerardiEtAl1973](#ref-GerardiEtAl1973)]. Our estimates include local occupation and tail-continuity bounds uniform in the previously chosen attraction ranges. The Kac limit itself fixes each finite reference before sending the new range to infinity. The local quadratic energy estimates belong to Ruelle’s superstability framework (Ruelle 1970) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Ruelle1970](#ref-Ruelle1970)]. Convex methods for constructing coexistence have antecedents in Israel’s tangent-functional argument (Israel 1975, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Israel1975](#ref-Israel1975)] and Ruelle’s restoration of lattice-gas coexistence by small changes of long-range pair couplings and chemical potential (Ruelle 1977, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ruelle1977Coexistence](#ref-Ruelle1977Coexistence)]. Here the scaled perturbation argument keeps three separated groups of joint pressure slopes, and hence a common ball of slopes, through infinitely many physical approximations.

A companion construction (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAIAlgebraic2026](#ref-OpenAIAlgebraic2026)] gives a bounded continuous radial potential with $|\phi(r)|\le Cr^{-3-1/32}$ for $r\ge1$ and a common temperature singularity throughout a density interval. That result uses a different potential and a quantitative range estimate; the present proof retains the divergent core and proves its own integrable-tail conclusion.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### How the construction works

For orientation, fix a scale $t>0$ and write the activity as $e^{th}$. The normalized pressure is the thermodynamic limit of $$\frac1{tL^3}\log\sum_{N\ge0}e^{thN}Z_{\Lambda_L,N}^\Phi(\beta).$$ It is a convex function of $(\beta,h)$. A supporting vector $(s,d)$ at $(\beta_0,h_0)$ gives an affine plane of slope $(s,d)$ lying below that function and touching it there. The coordinate $d$ represents density. The proof will preserve a two-dimensional ball of these vectors, so that one density is compatible with more than one first-coordinate slope.

The proof follows four steps.

1.  **Build a stable reference gas.** The core allows pairs at an extremely small separation shell at no energy cost. At most four particles can have all their pair distances in that shell in $\mathbb R^3$. This geometric fact supplies a quadratic local lower bound for the energy. Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">2</a> uses that bound to establish the canonical thermodynamic limit and its dual grand-canonical pressure. The scaled function $\beta f_\Phi(\beta,\rho)/t$ is concave in inverse temperature.

2.  **Create three competing pressure branches.** For a large scale parameter $t$, the grand-canonical activity is $e^{th}$. The reference pressure has three limiting branches, corresponding to empty space, well-separated particles, and tetrahedral clusters. Adding a spread-out radial attraction contributes a positive quadratic density term to the variational formula for pressure. Sections <a href="#sec:kac" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:kac">3</a> and <a href="#sec:triple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:triple">4</a> establish this comparison and produce a pressure with three noncollinear groups of supporting slopes.

3.  **Preserve those slopes at finite ranges.** Uniform approximation alone can smooth a corner. Section <a href="#sec:convex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:convex">5</a> therefore works at the scale of each new attractive mass: the physical approximation error is smaller than that scale, while the density quadratic separates the three groups. At each stage this yields a nearby point with the same ball of supporting slopes.

4.  **Pass to one potential and a density interval.** Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">6</a> chooses the attraction masses before the corresponding approximation ranges. Their summability gives one admissible potential, and summable shifts give one limiting pressure point. The common ball of supporting slopes passes to that physical pressure. Every horizontal slice near the center of the ball fixes a positive density and gives two different inverse-temperature slopes of the canonical free energy at the same critical point.

The elementary convex facts used in these steps are proved in Appendix <a href="#app:convex-facts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:convex-facts">7</a>. They include local uniform convergence, partial conjugacy, and representation of a subdifferential by limits of ordinary gradients.

<a id="sec:model"></a>
### A stable class with four-particle clusters

We first construct a class of potentials for which thermodynamic limits exist and the required density bounds are uniform in the ranges of the attractive interactions. The positive core permits small clusters of at most four particles without an energy cost. Its quadratic penalty for larger local populations will control every attractive term added later.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The packing density and the core

<a id="eq:packing-density"></a>
Let $M_L$ be the largest cardinality of a subset of $\Lambda_L=[-L/2,L/2]^3$ whose distinct points have distance at least one. The number is finite, and compactness gives a maximizing configuration. There is a constant $$p=\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{M_L}{L^3}\in(0,\infty),
 \qquad A=\frac{3}{2p}.
 \label{eq:packing-density}$$ Here we need only the existence of $p$, not its numerical value. Disjoint balls of radius $1/2$ around the packing points lie in the cube of side $L+1$, giving an upper bound on $M_L/L^3$ for $L\ge1$. A unit lattice gives a positive lower bound. For convergence, repeat a maximizing packing in a cube of side $s$ with period $s+1$ in each coordinate. Points in different copies are still separated by at least one, so $$\liminf_{L\to\infty}\frac{M_L}{L^3}
 \ge \frac{M_s}{(s+1)^3}.$$ Letting $s\to\infty$ along a sequence realizing the limsup proves <a href="#eq:packing-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:packing-density">[eq:packing-density]</a>.

Fix $$\delta=10^{-3},\qquad b=\frac14.$$ For a parameter $t>1$, put $E=t^2$ and $e_t=e^{-t}$. Define the radial nonnegative core, with $r=|x|$, by <a id="eq:core"></a>$$\psi_t(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 +\infty,&r=0,\\
 E+(e_t/r)^{12},&0<r<e_t,\\
 0,&e_t\le r\le(1+\delta)e_t,\\
 E,&(1+\delta)e_t<r<1,\\
 0,&r\ge1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:core}$$ Thus particles can approach one another without paying the energy $E$ only when their separation lies in a narrow shell of radius $e_t$. We allow $t$ to vary until the initial pressure configuration has been constructed; thereafter one sufficiently large finite value will be fixed.

<a id="eq:physical-class"></a>
Choose a nonnegative radial function $w\in C^1(\mathbb R^3)$, supported in the unit ball, strictly positive on the closed ball of radius $1/2$, and normalized by $\int_{\mathbb R^3}w(x)\,\,\mathrm dx=1$. For $R\ge1$ set $w_R(x)=R^{-3}w(x/R)$. Our class consists of $$\varphi=\psi_t-S,
 \qquad S=t\sum_j\sigma_j w_{R_j},
 \qquad R_j\ge1,\quad \sigma_j\ge0,
 \quad \sum_j\sigma_j\le A+1.
 \label{eq:physical-class}$$ The sum may be finite, empty, or countably infinite. Since $\|w_R\|_\infty\le\|w\|_\infty$ and $\|w_R\|_1=1$, it converges uniformly and in $L^1$ in the infinite case. In particular, $S$ is a bounded continuous nonnegative function. Every potential in this class is nonpositive at distances at least one. This sign will give a direct proof of the canonical thermodynamic limit.

<a id="eq:cell-row"></a>
Partition $\mathbb R^3$ into half-open cubes $C_i$ of side $b$, indexed by $i\in\mathbb Z^3$. For a finite configuration, let $n_i$ be the number of its particles in $C_i$. In a finite cube we intersect these cells with the cube; every resulting cell has volume at most $b^3$. Set $$J_{ij}=\sup_{x\in C_i,\,y\in C_j}S(x-y),
 \qquad \sum_jJ_{ij}\le M,
 \qquad M=c_b t(A+1),
 \label{eq:cell-row}$$ where $c_b$ depends only on $b$ and $w$. To see the row bound, first use one kernel $w_R$. Each nonzero cell supremum is at most $\|w\|_\infty R^{-3}$, and there are at most $C_bR^3$ cells within distance $R$ of a given cell because $R\ge1$. Sum this bound over the coefficients $t\sigma_j$. Taking a supremum of a sum only decreases the result relative to the sum of suprema. The same proof therefore applies to infinitely many ranges and to cropped cells.

<a id="stmt-lem-shell-graph"></a>
<div id="lem:shell-graph" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (The shell graph). *No five points in $\mathbb R^3$ have all their mutual distances in $[e_t,(1+\delta)e_t]$. Consequently, a graph whose vertices are points of $\mathbb R^3$ and whose edges join pairs at these distances has at most $3n^2/8$ edges on any set of $n$ vertices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-shell-graph"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-shell-graph</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that five such points existed. Scale by $e_t^{-1}$ and form the Gram matrix $G$ of the four vectors from one point to the other four. Write $\varepsilon=2\delta+\delta^2$. The diagonal entries differ from 1 by at most $\varepsilon$. By the polarization identity, each off-diagonal entry differs from $1/2$ by at most $\varepsilon$. The matrix $G_0$ with diagonal 1 and off-diagonal $1/2$ has smallest eigenvalue $1/2$, whereas $$\|G-G_0\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le4\varepsilon
 =0.008004<\frac12.$$ Thus $G$ would have rank four, which is impossible for four vectors in $\mathbb R^3$.

For completeness, we give the weighted proof of the required graph bound, an instance of the Motzkin–Straus identity (Motzkin and Straus 1965, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MotzkinStraus1965](#ref-MotzkinStraus1965)]. For a graph with no complete subgraph on five vertices, maximize $$\sum_{\{i,j\}\text{ an edge}}a_i a_j
 \quad\text{over}\quad a_i\ge0,\qquad\sum_i a_i=1.$$ Choose a maximizer with the smallest possible support. If two vertices in its support are nonadjacent, transferring all their combined weight to the one with the larger weighted neighbor sum cannot decrease the objective. It decreases the support, a contradiction. The support is therefore a clique with at most four vertices. At this maximizer the objective is $$\frac12\left(1-\sum_i a_i^2\right)\le\frac38.$$ Applying this inequality to the weights $a_i=1/n$ gives the asserted edge count. The case $n=0$ is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-stability"></a>
<div id="prop:stability" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:superstable"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Uniform stability). *Put $A_0=E/8$ and $B_0=E/2$. Every potential in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a> satisfies, for every finite configuration, $$\sum_{\ell<k}\psi_t(x_\ell-x_k)
 \ge A_0\sum_i n_i^2-B_0N,
 \qquad
 U_N^\varphi\ge (A_0-M/2)\sum_i n_i^2-B_0N.
 \label{eq:superstable}$$ These inequalities remain valid if positive core interactions between members of a partition into unions of whole cells are deleted, or if any negative interactions are multiplied by factors in $[0,1]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-stability</summary>

*Proof.* The diameter of a cell is $\sqrt3/4<1$. Among the $n_i(n_i-1)/2$ pairs inside cell $i$, at most $3n_i^2/8$ lie in the shell by Lemma <a href="#lem:shell-graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shell-graph">2</a>. Every other pair has core energy at least $E$. Thus the core energy inside this cell is at least $$E\left(\frac{n_i(n_i-1)}2-\frac{3n_i^2}8\right)
 =A_0 n_i^2-B_0 n_i.$$ All remaining core interactions are nonnegative. Also, $$\sum_{\ell<k}S(x_\ell-x_k)
 \le\frac12\sum_{i,j}J_{ij}n_i n_j
 \le\frac M2\sum_i n_i^2,$$ where the first bound includes harmless self-particle terms and the second follows from symmetry, <a href="#eq:cell-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-row">[eq:cell-row]</a>, and $2n_i n_j\le n_i^2+n_j^2$. This proves <a href="#eq:superstable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:superstable">[eq:superstable]</a>. The permitted deletions preserve every core interaction within a small cell. Reducing a negative term only increases the energy, so the same argument proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:t-choice"></a>
From now on we require $t$ to be sufficiently large that $$2(1+\delta)e_t<1,
 \qquad A_0\ge8M,
 \qquad \kappa:=(A_0-M/2)b^3>10t(A+1).
 \label{eq:t-choice}$$ These conditions hold for all sufficiently large $t$, since $A_0$ is quadratic in $t$ and $M$ is linear in $t$. In particular every potential in the class is stable with constant $B_0$.

Each such potential is finite and locally bounded away from the origin and diverges to $+\infty$ there. Its tail is integrable, since $\varphi=-S$ outside the unit ball. If one coefficient is positive and its range is greater than 4, the potential is strictly negative uniformly on $1\le |x|\le2$: the corresponding rescaled kernel has a positive minimum there, while the core vanishes. Thus a potential with this additional simple condition has all the admissibility properties in the introduction. We next establish its thermodynamics, uniformly for the larger class <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The canonical limit for every density

Fix for the moment a value of $t$ satisfying <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a> and a potential $\varphi$ in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a>. Write $Z_{L,n}(\beta)=Z_{\Lambda_L,n}^\varphi(\beta)$, including $Z_{L,0}=1$, and set $$a_L(\beta,n)=-\frac{\log Z_{L,n}(\beta)}{tL^3}.$$ We use $0\log0=0$. The following proposition includes convergence along every sequence of particle numbers with a prescribed limiting density; that form will be needed in the long-range comparison.

<a id="stmt-prop-thermodynamics"></a>
<div id="prop:thermodynamics" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:canonical-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:coercivity"></a>
<a id="eq:pressure-duality"></a>
<a id="eq:partial-conjugacy"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Canonical and grand-canonical limits). *For every $\beta>0$ and $d\ge0$ there is a finite limit $$H_\varphi(\beta,d)
 =-\lim_{\substack{L\to\infty\\n/L^3\to d}}
       \frac{\log Z_{L,n}(\beta)}{tL^3},
 \qquad H_\varphi(\beta,0)=0.
 \label{eq:canonical-limit}$$ Extend $H_\varphi(\beta,d)$ by $+\infty$ for $d<0$. It is closed and convex in $d$, and finite and concave in $\beta$ for every $d\ge0$. Moreover, $$H_\varphi(\beta,d)
 \ge \frac{\beta\kappa}{t}d^2
       -\frac{\beta B_0}{t}d
       +\frac{d\log d-d}{t}
 \qquad(d\ge0).
 \label{eq:coercivity}$$ Let $\Omega=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb R$, with coordinates $(\beta,h)$, and define $$\Xi_L^\varphi(\beta,h)
   =\sum_{n\ge0}e^{thn}Z_{L,n}(\beta).$$ Then the finite convex pressure is $$Q_\varphi(\beta,h)
 =\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{\log\Xi_L^\varphi(\beta,h)}{tL^3}
 =\sup_{d\ge0}\{hd-H_\varphi(\beta,d)\}.
 \label{eq:pressure-duality}$$ The convergence is uniform on compact subsets of $\Omega$, and $$H_\varphi(\beta,d)
 =\sup_{h\in\mathbb R}\{hd-Q_\varphi(\beta,h)\}
 \qquad(d\in\mathbb R).
 \label{eq:partial-conjugacy}$$ The bounds above use the same constants for the entire class <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a> with the chosen $t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-thermodynamics"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-thermodynamics</summary>

*Proof.* We give the details of the canonical limit first. Two finite-volume bounds control the density adjustments in its construction.

<a id="eq:trial-bound"></a>
*A trial bound on bounded density intervals.* Fix $D_0<\infty$ and choose a grid spacing $q\in(0,1/2)$ with $q^{-3}>D_0+1$. For large $L$, at least $D_0L^3$ grid centers lie in $\Lambda_L$ at distance $q/8$ from its boundary. For $n\le D_0L^3$, put one particle in each of $n$ distinct balls of radius $q/8$ around such centers. Every pair has distance at least $3q/4$, and each particle has at most $Cq^{-3}$ neighbors at distance less than one. The core is bounded on $[3q/4,1]$, so the positive energy of these configurations is at most $E_q n$ for a finite constant $E_q$. We need not count the negative energy. The $n!$ assignments of the labeled particles to the balls have disjoint integration regions and cancel the canonical factorial. If $v_q>0$ is the volume of a ball, this gives $$Z_{L,n}(\beta)\ge v_q^n e^{-\beta E_q n}.$$ Consequently $$a_L(\beta,n)\le C_{D_0,\beta}
 \qquad(0\le n\le D_0L^3, L\text{ sufficiently large}).
 \label{eq:trial-bound}$$ The constant is uniform over the physical class, and can be chosen uniformly for $\beta$ in a compact subset of $(0,\infty)$.

<a id="eq:finite-coercivity"></a>
*A lower bound.* There are at most $(L/b+2)^3$ cropped cells in $\Lambda_L$. Cauchy–Schwarz in <a href="#eq:superstable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:superstable">[eq:superstable]</a> and the inequality $n!\ge(n/e)^n$ therefore give, with $d_L=n/L^3$, $$a_L(\beta,n)
 \ge \frac{\beta\kappa}{t(1+2b/L)^3}d_L^2
       -\frac{\beta B_0}{t}d_L
       +\frac{d_L\log d_L-d_L}{t}.
 \label{eq:finite-coercivity}$$ Dropping the positive quadratic term also gives the same inequality with only its last two terms. In particular, $Z_{L,n}$ is positive and finite, and $a_L$ is bounded above and below when the density stays in a fixed bounded interval and $L$ is large.

*Convergence at a positive density.* Fix $\beta>0$ and $d>0$. The increasing infima $$I_j=\inf\left\{a_L(\beta,n):
       L\ge j,\ \left|n/L^3-d\right|\le1/j\right\}$$ converge to a finite number $I$. Select $\ell_j\ge j$ and $n_j$ in this set such that $a_{\ell_j}(\beta,n_j)\le I_j+1/j$. Then $n_j/\ell_j^3\to d$ and $a_{\ell_j}(\beta,n_j)\to I$. Every sequence with $L\to\infty$ and $n/L^3\to d$ has liminf at least $I_j$ for each $j$, and hence at least $I$.

<a id="eq:separated-boxes"></a>
To prove the reverse inequality, tile part of a large cube by $$k=\left\lfloor\frac{L}{\ell_j+2}\right\rfloor^3$$ cubes of side $\ell_j$, leaving gaps of width two. Every interaction between distinct cubes is nonpositive. For any prescribed counts $m_1,\ldots,m_k$, restricting the integral to these cubes gives $$Z_{L,\sum_i m_i}(\beta)
 \ge\prod_{i=1}^k Z_{\ell_j,m_i}(\beta).
 \label{eq:separated-boxes}$$ Indeed there are $(\sum_i m_i)!/\prod_i m_i!$ assignments of labels to the cubes, and these give exactly the factorials on the right.

Start with $n_j$ particles in every small cube. Define a higher count $n_{\mathrm{hi}}=\lfloor(4d+4)\ell_j^3\rfloor$ and the diluted densities $$d_b=\frac{n_j}{(\ell_j+2)^3},\qquad
 d_{\mathrm{hi}}=\frac{n_{\mathrm{hi}}}{(\ell_j+2)^3}.$$ For large $j$, $n_j>0$, $n_{\mathrm{hi}}>n_j$, and $d_b\to d$, $d_{\mathrm{hi}}\to4d+4$. If a target count $n$ exceeds $kn_j$, change some cubes to $n_{\mathrm{hi}}$; if it is smaller, empty some cubes. One additional intermediate count realizes $n$ exactly. For fixed sufficiently large $j$, this is possible for all large $L$ with $n/L^3\to d$. The limiting upper fraction of changed cubes is at most $$c_j=\max\left\{
 \frac{(d-d_b)_+}{d_{\mathrm{hi}}-d_b},
 \frac{(d_b-d)_+}{d_b}\right\},
 \qquad c_j\longrightarrow0.$$ All new counts are bounded by $(4d+4)\ell_j^3$, so their values of $a_{\ell_j}$ have a common upper bound by <a href="#eq:trial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-bound">[eq:trial-bound]</a>. The baseline values are bounded in absolute value. Applying <a href="#eq:separated-boxes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-boxes">[eq:separated-boxes]</a>, taking the large-volume limit with $j$ fixed, and then letting $j\to\infty$ therefore gives $$\limsup_{\substack{L\to\infty\\n/L^3\to d}}a_L(\beta,n)
 \le\lim_{j\to\infty}
 \left(\frac{\ell_j}{\ell_j+2}\right)^3
 \bigl(a_{\ell_j}(\beta,n_j)+C c_j\bigr)=I.$$ The single intermediate cube contributes zero in the first limit. This proves the all-sequence convergence for $d>0$.

*Zero density and convexity.* When $n/L^3\to0$, the linear lower bound derived above has limit zero. For an upper bound, fix a sufficiently large side $\ell$ and use separated cubes as in <a href="#eq:separated-boxes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-boxes">[eq:separated-boxes]</a>, with empty baseline. Fill some cubes with $\lfloor\ell^3\rfloor$ particles and at most one with an intermediate count. Their number is $o(L^3)$, so <a href="#eq:trial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-bound">[eq:trial-bound]</a> gives a limiting upper bound zero. Thus $H_\varphi(\beta,0)=0$.

For convexity, take $0\le d_1<d_2$ and a density strictly between them. For a large fixed $\ell$, use counts $\lfloor d_1\ell^3\rfloor$ and $\lfloor d_2\ell^3\rfloor$ in the separated cubes. The desired density lies strictly between their diluted densities for all sufficiently large $\ell$. Choose their proportions to attain the target count, with at most one intermediate cube. Inequality <a href="#eq:separated-boxes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-boxes">[eq:separated-boxes]</a>, followed first by $L\to\infty$ and then by $\ell\to\infty$, gives the convexity inequality for $H_\varphi(\beta,\cdot)$. The endpoint cases of that inequality are immediate.

The function is finite and convex on $[0,\infty)$, hence continuous on $(0,\infty)$. The linear lower bound gives lower semicontinuity at zero; since its value there is zero, extension by $+\infty$ to negative densities is a closed convex function. For each fixed density, Hölder’s inequality makes $-\log Z_{L,n}(\beta)$ concave in $\beta$. Passage to the limit gives finite concavity of $H_\varphi(\cdot,d)$ on $(0,\infty)$. Finally <a href="#eq:finite-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-coercivity">[eq:finite-coercivity]</a> gives <a href="#eq:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coercivity">[eq:coercivity]</a>.

We have established the canonical limit with enough control over particle number to pass to the grand-canonical ensemble. In particular, the density variable in the next maximization will not require a separate approximation of the canonical free energy.

*Pressure and conjugacy.* On a compact subset of $\Omega$, <a href="#eq:finite-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-coercivity">[eq:finite-coercivity]</a> implies that, for sufficiently large $D$ and all large $L$, $$e^{thn}Z_{L,n}(\beta)\le e^{-c n^2/L^3}
 \qquad(n>DL^3)$$ with $c>0$ independent of the potential in the class. The sum of these terms is exponentially small in $L^3$, whereas the term $n=0$ is one. Among the remaining at most $\lfloor DL^3\rfloor+1$ terms, choose one of maximal size. Every subsequence has a further subsequence on which its density converges to some $d\in[0,D]$. The all-sequence canonical limit identifies the limiting exponent as $hd-H_\varphi(\beta,d)$. Conversely, the term $n=\lfloor dL^3\rfloor$ supplies this exponent as a lower bound for every $d\ge0$. This proves <a href="#eq:pressure-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-duality">[eq:pressure-duality]</a>.

The finite-volume logarithms are finite and jointly convex in $(\beta,h)$, by Hölder’s inequality applied to their defining integrals and sums. The limit is finite by the density bound, and it is nonnegative because the empty configuration is allowed. Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-convex-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-convex-convergence">15</a>, the finite-dimensional convex convergence principle of (Rockafellar and Wets 1998, Corollary 7.18) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[RockafellarWets1998](#ref-RockafellarWets1998)], upgrades pointwise convergence to uniform convergence on compact subsets of the open convex set $\Omega$. Finally, the closed convexity in density proved above permits the one-dimensional Fenchel biconjugacy identity, proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:elementary-conjugacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:elementary-conjugacy">16</a> and treated in (Rockafellar and Wets 1998, Theorem 11.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[RockafellarWets1998](#ref-RockafellarWets1998)], yielding <a href="#eq:partial-conjugacy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:partial-conjugacy">[eq:partial-conjugacy]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The quadratic density transform

<a id="eq:transform"></a>
We will compare a physical pressure with the pressure obtained by adding an attraction whose range tends to infinity. The appropriate variational expression is a quadratic modification in the density variable. For any physical pressure $Q=Q_\varphi$, write $$H_Q(\beta,d)=\sup_{h\in\mathbb R}\{hd-Q(\beta,h)\}.$$ By <a href="#eq:partial-conjugacy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:partial-conjugacy">[eq:partial-conjugacy]</a>, this is precisely the canonical function $H_\varphi$, including its value $+\infty$ for $d<0$. For $0\le\sigma\le A+1$, define $$(T_\sigma Q)(\beta,h)
 =\sup_{d\ge0}
   \left\{hd-H_Q(\beta,d)+\frac{\beta\sigma}{2}d^2\right\}.
 \label{eq:transform}$$ This definition does not assert that $T_\sigma Q$ is itself the pressure of a finite-range pair potential. The next section proves the approximation by physical pressures that we shall use.

<a id="stmt-prop-transform"></a>
<div id="prop:transform" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Bounds for the density transform). *Let $t$ satisfy <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a>. For every physical pressure $Q_\varphi$ in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a> and every $\sigma\in[0,A+1]$, the function $T_\sigma Q_\varphi$ is finite and convex on $\Omega$, nonnegative, and nondecreasing in $h$. The supremum in <a href="#eq:transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform">[eq:transform]</a> is attained. For every compact $W\subset\Omega$, there is $D_W<\infty$, depending only on $W,t,b,w,A$, such that every maximizing density belongs to $[0,D_W]$, uniformly over all potentials in the class and all these values of $\sigma$. The transformed pressures are also uniformly bounded on $W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-transform"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-transform</summary>

*Proof.* The value at $d=0$ is zero. By <a href="#eq:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coercivity">[eq:coercivity]</a>, the objective in <a href="#eq:transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform">[eq:transform]</a> is bounded above by <a id="eq:transform-coercivity"></a>$$hd-\beta\left(\frac\kappa t-\frac\sigma2\right)d^2
       +\frac{\beta B_0}{t}d
       -\frac{d\log d-d}{t}.
 \label{eq:transform-coercivity}$$ Condition <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a> gives a uniform positive lower bound on $\kappa/t-\sigma/2$. On a compact set of $(\beta,h)$ with $\beta$ bounded away from zero, the right side is therefore negative for every sufficiently large $d$, uniformly in the potential and in $\sigma$. This proves the common density bound and the finite upper bound for the supremum. The objective is upper semicontinuous in $d$, since $H_Q(\beta,\cdot)$ is lower semicontinuous. It attains its maximum on the resulting compact interval.

For each fixed $d\ge0$, the function in braces in <a href="#eq:transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform">[eq:transform]</a> is convex in $(\beta,h)$: the canonical function is concave in $\beta$, and the other terms are affine. Their supremum is convex. Its monotonicity in $h$ follows from $d\ge0$, and its nonnegativity from the zero-density choice. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:free-energy-normalization"></a>
The normalizations are now fixed. In particular, for every positive density the free energy in the statement of the problem is $$f_\varphi(\beta,d)=\frac{t}{\beta}H_\varphi(\beta,d).
 \label{eq:free-energy-normalization}$$ For a real-valued function $F$ on a compact set $K$, write $\|F\|_K=\sup_{x\in K}|F(x)|$. All limits and finiteness statements in this section hold for every $\beta>0$, including for infinite sums in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a>. The remaining task is to choose the attractive ranges and coefficients so that one of these canonical functions has two different temperature slopes at one fixed positive density.

<a id="sec:kac"></a>
### Uniform estimates and the Kac approximation

The next step relates a physical long-range attraction to the transform $T_\sigma$ in <a href="#eq:transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform">[eq:transform]</a>. Throughout this section, $t$ satisfies <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a> and is fixed. All uniform estimates concern the physical class <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a> at this fixed $t$; they need not remain uniform as $t$ tends to infinity. The approximation is a form of the Lebowitz–Penrose limit, whose extension to superstable reference interactions is classical (J. L. Lebowitz and Penrose 1966; Gerardi et al. 1973) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[LebowitzPenrose1966](#ref-LebowitzPenrose1966), [GerardiEtAl1973](#ref-GerardiEtAl1973)]. We give the proof because we will need constants independent of the attraction ranges, including in auxiliary systems with some positive interactions removed.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Local moments and continuity in the attractive tail

Recall the cells $C_i$ of side $b$, their occupation numbers $n_i$, and the attractive interaction bounds $J_{ij}$ from <a href="#eq:cell-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-row">[eq:cell-row]</a>. In a finite cube, consider the grand canonical measure with activity $e^{th}$. We also allow two modifications: positive core interactions may be deleted between boxes that are unions of whole cells, and negative pair interactions may be multiplied by measurable symmetric factors in $[0,1]$. Every original cell retains its internal core interaction. Consequently, the lower bound <a href="#eq:superstable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:superstable">[eq:superstable]</a> continues to hold.

<a id="stmt-prop-moments"></a>
<div id="prop:moments" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Uniform local moments). *Fix a compact set $\mathcal K\subset\Omega$. For all potentials in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a>, all finite cubes, and all the modified systems just described, there is a constant $C_{\mathcal K}$ such that $$\mathbb E_{\beta,h}[n_i n_j]\le C_{\mathcal K}
 \qquad ((\beta,h)\in\mathcal K)$$ for every pair of cells meeting the cube. The constant is independent of the number and ranges of the attractive summands.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-moments</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on the configuration $Y$ outside $C_i$, and put $$A_i(Y)=\sum_{j\ne i}J_{ij}n_j,\qquad c=\frac{\beta A_0}{4}.$$ The interactions involving the $n$ particles inside $C_i$ are bounded below by $$(A_0-J_{ii}/2)n^2-B_0n-nA_i.$$ The empty cell contributes one to the conditional partition function. Its volume is at most $b^3$, also when cropped by the cube boundary. It follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[e^{c n_i^2}\mid Y]
 &\le
 \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{b^{3n}}{n!}
 \exp\!\left(cn^2+thn-\beta(A_0-J_{ii}/2)n^2
                  +\beta B_0n+\beta nA_i\right)\\
 &\le C_1\exp(\beta A_i^2/A_0).
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $\beta nA_i\le\beta A_0n^2/4+\beta A_i^2/A_0$; after this estimate the remaining negative quadratic coefficient is at least $$\beta(A_0/2-M/2)\ge 7\beta A_0/16.$$ The resulting one-variable series is bounded uniformly on $\mathcal K$. The conditional bound still depends on $Y$. We remove that dependence by a finite-volume bootstrap.

Let $F_i=\mathbb E[e^{c n_i^2}]$. These numbers are finite before the bootstrap: multiplying the grand canonical integrand by $e^{cn_i^2}$ leaves a strictly negative quadratic in all cell counts, by <a href="#eq:superstable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:superstable">[eq:superstable]</a>. Cauchy–Schwarz with weights $J_{ij}$ gives $$A_i^2\le M\sum_{j\ne i}J_{ij}n_j^2.$$ If $M>0$, set $\theta_{ij}=J_{ij}/M$ and $\alpha=4(M/A_0)^2<1$. Then $c\alpha=\beta M^2/A_0$. The row weights sum to at most one; place any missing weight on a value zero. Convexity of the exponential and then concavity of $x^\alpha$ give $$F_i\le C_1\left(1+\sup_j F_j^\alpha\right).$$ There are finitely many cells, so their maximum is bounded by a constant depending only on $C_1,\alpha$. When $M=0$, the conditional estimate already gives $F_i\le C_1$. Since $\beta$ is bounded away from zero on $\mathcal K$, this proves uniform second moments. A final Cauchy–Schwarz inequality proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

This is a direct version of the local-occupancy estimates associated with superstability (Ruelle 1970) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Ruelle1970](#ref-Ruelle1970)]. Its extra uniformity will control both the infinite tail and the boundary errors in the Kac comparison.

<a id="stmt-prop-tail"></a>
<div id="prop:tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:pressure-interpolation"></a>
<a id="eq:tail-continuity"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Attractive perturbations). *Fix a compact set $\mathcal K\subset\Omega$. For two of the preceding finite-volume systems differing by a negative interaction, let $J^{\mathrm{diff}}_{ij}$ be cellwise suprema of its absolute value. If the linear interpolation stays within the same attractive mass bound, then $$\label{eq:pressure-interpolation}
 |\log\Xi(1)-\log\Xi(0)|
 \le \frac{\beta C_{\mathcal K}}2\sum_{i,j}J^{\mathrm{diff}}_{ij}
 \qquad ((\beta,h)\in\mathcal K).$$ In particular, for $$\varphi=\psi_t-t\sum_{j\ge1}\sigma_jw_{R_j},
 \qquad
 \varphi_n=\psi_t-t\sum_{j\le n}\sigma_jw_{R_j},$$ there is a range-independent constant $C'_{\mathcal K}$ such that $$\label{eq:tail-continuity}
 \|Q_\varphi-Q_{\varphi_n}\|_{\mathcal K}
 \le C'_{\mathcal K}\sum_{j>n}\sigma_j .$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tail</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiate the logarithm of the grand partition function along the interpolation. The absolute derivative is at most $$\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i,j}J^{\mathrm{diff}}_{ij}\,
                 \mathbb E[n_i n_j].$$ Including particle self pairs only enlarges this bound. Differentiation under the integrals and sums is valid by the common negative quadratic in the finitely many cell counts. Proposition <a href="#prop:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:moments">6</a> and integration over the interpolation parameter prove <a href="#eq:pressure-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-interpolation">[eq:pressure-interpolation]</a>.

For the tail difference, each row sum is at most $c_b t\sum_{j>n}\sigma_j$, and the number of cells is at most $(L/b+2)^3$. Divide <a href="#eq:pressure-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-interpolation">[eq:pressure-interpolation]</a> by $tL^3$, use the pressure limit in Proposition <a href="#prop:thermodynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:thermodynamics">4</a>, and let $L\to\infty$. The bounds are uniform on $\mathcal K$, proving <a href="#eq:tail-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-continuity">[eq:tail-continuity]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### One long-range attraction

<a id="stmt-prop-kac"></a>
<div id="prop:kac" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:kac-limit"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Physical Kac approximation). *Let $$\varphi=\psi_t-t\sum_{j=1}^k\sigma_jw_{R_j}$$ have finitely many attractive summands, with the empty sum allowed. Fix $\sigma\ge0$ such that $\sum_{j=1}^k\sigma_j+\sigma\le A+1$. Then $$\label{eq:kac-limit}
 Q_{\varphi-t\sigma w_R}\longrightarrow T_\sigma Q_\varphi
 \quad\text{as }R\to\infty$$ uniformly on compact subsets of $\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-kac"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-kac</summary>

*Proof.* Fix the reference potential, and let $R_0$ bound its interaction range. All constants depending on $R_0$ are fixed during this proof. Partition a cube of side $L=mu$ into $m^3$ boxes of side $$u=b\left\lfloor R^{1/2}/b\right\rfloor.$$ Translate the $b$-cell lattice, if necessary, to align it with the outside cube; all preceding estimates are invariant under this translation. The $u$-boxes are then unions of whole $b$-cells. For each $R$, we first let $m\to\infty$; only afterward do we let $R\to\infty$. In particular $u\to\infty$, $u/R\to0$, and eventually $u\gg R_0$.

<a id="eq:kac-lower-row"></a>
Write $B_z$ for the box centered at $z$, and define $$K^+_{zz'}=\sup_{x\in B_z,\ y\in B_{z'}}w_R(x-y),
 \qquad
 K^-_{zz'}=\bigl(w_R(z-z')-Cu/R^4\bigr)_+.$$ Choose the fixed constant $C$ large enough that $K^-_{zz'}$ is at most the corresponding infimum. The $C^1$ bound on $w$ and its compact support imply, for some $\eta_R=O(u/R)\to0$, <a id="eq:kac-rows"></a>$$\label{eq:kac-rows}
 u^3\sum_{z'}K^+_{zz'}\le1+\eta_R.$$ If $z$ is farther than $R+3u$ from the outside cube boundary, then $$\label{eq:kac-lower-row}
 \sum_{z'}K^-_{zz'}\ge(1-\eta_R)u^{-3}.$$ Here the sums are over the finite center grid in the cube. To check these estimates, compare $u^3\sum_{z'}w_R(z-z')$ with $\int w_R=1$. The oscillation in each grid cube is $O(uR^{-4})$, and only $O((R/u)^3)$ cubes meet the support enlarged by $3u$. The total error after multiplication by $u^3$ is $O(u/R)$. The same argument controls the suprema and the truncated lower kernel. Also $K^\pm_{zz'}\le C R^{-3}$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Upper bound.

Delete all positive core interactions between the $u$-boxes. This increases the partition function. Remove next the reference attractive interactions between these boxes, keeping the new Kac attraction in place. By Proposition <a href="#prop:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tail">7</a>, this second change costs at most $C_{\mathcal K,R_0}L^3/u$ in the logarithm. Indeed only original cells within $R_0+2b$ of a box boundary have a nonzero difference row, and there are $O_{R_0}(L^3/u)$ such cells. Proposition <a href="#prop:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:moments">6</a> applies throughout this interpolation.

<a id="eq:kac-upper"></a>
Write $n_z$ for the count in the box centered at $z$. The long-range pair sum is at most $$\frac12\sum_{z,z'}K^+_{zz'}n_zn_{z'}
 \le \frac{1+\eta_R}{2u^3}\sum_z n_z^2,$$ where the last step uses symmetry and $n_zn_{z'}\le(n_z^2+n_{z'}^2)/2$. The first bound includes self pairs, which is harmless for this upper estimate of an attractive Boltzmann factor. The remaining reference interactions are confined to individual boxes. Summing the assignments of particle labels cancels the global canonical factorial and gives $$\label{eq:kac-upper}
 \Xi_{mu}^{\varphi-t\sigma w_R}
 \le
 \exp(C_{\mathcal K,R_0}L^3/u)
 \left[
 \sum_{n\ge0}e^{thn}Z_{u,n}^{\varphi}(\beta)
 \exp\!\left(\frac{\beta t\sigma(1+\eta_R)}{2u^3}n^2\right)
 \right]^{m^3}.$$

The finite-volume bound <a href="#eq:finite-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-coercivity">[eq:finite-coercivity]</a> and $\kappa>10t(A+1)$ leave the effective energy coefficient $$\frac{\kappa}{(1+2b/u)^3}-\frac{t\sigma(1+\eta_R)}2>0$$ uniformly away from zero for all sufficiently large $R$. Consequently counts $n/u^3>D$, for sufficiently large $D$, are suppressed by a uniform negative quadratic. The sum of these high-count terms is exponentially small compared with the empty term. Among the remaining $O(u^3)$ terms, every maximizing subsequence has a further subsequence with $n/u^3\to d\in[0,D]$. The all-sequence limit <a href="#eq:canonical-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:canonical-limit">[eq:canonical-limit]</a> then gives $$\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac1{tu^3}
 \log\!\left[
 \sum_{n\ge0}e^{thn}Z_{u,n}^{\varphi}(\beta)
 \exp\!\left(\frac{\beta t\sigma(1+\eta_R)}{2u^3}n^2\right)
 \right]
 =\sup_{d\ge0}\left\{hd-H_\varphi(\beta,d)
                         +\frac{\beta\sigma d^2}{2}\right\}.$$ The logarithm of the number of low-count terms divided by $u^3$ vanishes. This proves the equality by the preceding upper subsequence argument and by taking $n=\lfloor du^3\rfloor$ for the lower bound. Taking first $m\to\infty$ in <a href="#eq:kac-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kac-upper">[eq:kac-upper]</a> and then $R\to\infty$ proves the desired upper estimate.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Lower bound.

Place a cube of side $s=u-2$ at each of the same centers, and require exactly $n=\lfloor ds^3\rfloor$ particles in every smaller cube, where $d\ge0$ is fixed. The gaps have width two. Thus all reference interactions between distinct smaller cubes are nonpositive and can be discarded in a lower bound on the partition function. Let $m_{\mathrm{int}}$ be the number of centers satisfying the interior condition in <a href="#eq:kac-lower-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kac-lower-row">[eq:kac-lower-row]</a>. The Kac pair sum obeys $$\sum_{\ell<j}w_R(x_\ell-x_j)
 \ge \frac12m_{\mathrm{int}}n^2u^{-3}(1-\eta_R)
                      -\frac12m^3nCR^{-3}.$$ The last term removes the particle self pairs included in the diagonal box contribution. The count constraint and the canonical factorials therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 \Xi_{mu}^{\varphi-t\sigma w_R}
 \ge{}&
 \left(e^{thn}Z_{s,n}^{\varphi}(\beta)\right)^{m^3}\\
 &{}\times
 \exp\!\left\{\frac{\beta t\sigma}{2}
 \left[m_{\mathrm{int}}n^2u^{-3}(1-\eta_R)-m^3nCR^{-3}\right]\right\}.
\end{aligned}$$ For fixed $R$, $m_{\mathrm{int}}/m^3\to1$ as $m\to\infty$. Next $R\to\infty$ gives $s/u\to1$, $n/s^3\to d$, and a vanishing self-pair error. By <a href="#eq:canonical-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:canonical-limit">[eq:canonical-limit]</a>, the resulting pressure lower bound is $$hd-H_\varphi(\beta,d)+\frac{\beta\sigma d^2}{2}.$$ Take the supremum over $d\ge0$.

We have proved pointwise convergence in <a href="#eq:kac-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kac-limit">[eq:kac-limit]</a>. The functions on both sides are finite and convex on the open convex set $\Omega$, by Propositions <a href="#prop:thermodynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:thermodynamics">4</a> and <a href="#prop:transform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transform">5</a>. Pointwise convergence of finite convex functions to a finite convex function is uniform on compact subsets, which completes the proof. ◻

</details>

The proposition uses boxes only in its upper and lower estimates. Both sides of <a href="#eq:kac-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kac-limit">[eq:kac-limit]</a> are built from the full radial reference interaction. At every later stage we will fix the finite reference first and then choose a sufficiently large new range; no uniform rate over changing reference ranges is required.

<a id="sec:triple"></a>
### An initial triple of supporting slopes

We now choose $t$ so that the ideal transform $T_AQ_{\psi_t}$ has three separated groups of supporting slopes. The core permits zero-energy clusters of sizes $m=1,2,3,4$. After one root is positioned, each of the other $m-1$ particles has a relative position in a region of volume proportional to $e^{-3t}$. With activity $e^{th}$, the leading exponent per cluster, divided by $t$, is therefore $$mh-3(m-1).$$ This expression is affine in $m$, so its maximum over the four allowed sizes occurs at $m=1$ or $m=4$. Using the limiting packing density $p$, together with the empty configuration, gives the three branches in the next proposition. The proof below controls configurations that pay a positive core energy as well.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The free-cluster limit

Write $q_t=Q_{\psi_t}$. In this section we temporarily let $t\to\infty$; one value will be fixed at its end. The packing density $p$ and $A=3/(2p)$ retain their definitions from Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">2</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-free-pressure"></a>
<div id="prop:free-pressure" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:free-pressure-limit"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Reference pressure). *Uniformly on compact subsets of $\Omega$, $$\label{eq:free-pressure-limit}
 q_t(\beta,h)\longrightarrow
 q_*(h):=\max(0,ph,4ph-9p).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-free-pressure"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-free-pressure</summary>

*Proof.* Call a configuration free if every pair distance below one lies in $[e^{-t},(1+\delta)e^{-t}]$. Join points at distance below one. A two-edge path has endpoint distance at most $2(1+\delta)e^{-t}<1$; freeness makes the endpoints adjacent. Thus connected components are complete graphs. The geometric estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:shell-graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shell-graph">2</a> bounds their sizes by four. Representatives from different components are separated by at least one, so their number is at most $M_L$.

<a id="eq:free-enumeration"></a>
Let $V=L^3$, and let $\Xi_{\mathrm{free}}$ denote the grand configurational integral restricted to free configurations. Root each component and integrate its root over volume $V$. Every other point in the component lies in a ball of volume at most $c_0e^{-3t}$ about the root. The labeled block enumeration, followed by division by the canonical factorial, gives the upper bound $$\label{eq:free-enumeration}
 \Xi_{\mathrm{free}}\le
 \sum_{k=0}^{M_L}\frac{(V\Theta_t)^k}{k!},
 \qquad
 \Theta_t=\sum_{m=1}^4
 \frac{e^{thm}(c_0e^{-3t})^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}.$$ For completeness, ordered component sizes give a factor $1/(k!\prod m_i!)$; allowing any of the $m_i$ labels to be a root replaces $m_i!$ by $(m_i-1)!$ and only increases the count. Ignoring separation between the roots also enlarges the integral.

<a id="eq:bad-particle-factor"></a>
A bad edge is a pair at distance below one but outside the allowed shell. If there are $B$ bad edges, their endpoint set $S$ has size at most $2B$, its complement is free, and the core energy is at least $t^2B$. Hence, pointwise on labeled configurations, $$e^{-\beta U^{\psi_t}}
 \le \sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}}
        e^{-\beta t^2|S|/2}\,
        {\bf1}_{\{\text{the complement of }S\text{ is free}\}}.$$ The term corresponding to the actual bad-edge endpoint set proves this inequality; zero Boltzmann factors cause no difficulty. Summing over particle numbers and integrating the removed particles gives $$\label{eq:bad-particle-factor}
 \Xi_L^{\psi_t}
 \le \exp\!\left(Ve^{th-\beta t^2/2}\right)\Xi_{\mathrm{free}}.$$

Now $$\frac1t\log\Theta_t
 \longrightarrow
 \max_{1\le m\le4}\{mh-3(m-1)\}
 =\max(h,4h-9).$$ The convergence is uniform for bounded $h$. For $u=k/V$, the factorial estimate $k!\ge(k/e)^k$ bounds the remaining normalized logarithm in <a href="#eq:free-enumeration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-enumeration">[eq:free-enumeration]</a> by $u(1-\log u)/t$, with value zero at $u=0$. It is uniformly $o(1)$ for bounded $u$ as $t\to\infty$. There are only $M_L+1=O(V)$ terms. Take $L\to\infty$ first and use $M_L/V\to p$. The extra pressure in <a href="#eq:bad-particle-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bad-particle-factor">[eq:bad-particle-factor]</a> is $e^{th-\beta t^2/2}/t$, which tends to zero uniformly on compact subsets of $\Omega$. This proves the upper limit in <a href="#eq:free-pressure-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-pressure-limit">[eq:free-pressure-limit]</a>.

For the lower limit, fix $\ell>0$ and $\eta>0$. Dilate an optimal $M_\ell$-packing by $1+\eta$, and repeat it with cubic period $$P=(1+\eta)(\ell+2).$$ In a cube of side $mP$, the $k=m^3M_\ell$ resulting centers have mutual distances at least $1+\eta$ and a fixed positive margin from the outside boundary. At each center put either one particle or four particles, using the same choice $m_0\in\{1,4\}$ everywhere. The root ranges over a ball of fixed volume $v_0>0$, small compared with the separation margin. For $m_0=4$, put the other particles in relative balls of volume $v_1e^{-3t}$ about the three other vertices of a regular tetrahedron of side $(1+\delta/2)e^{-t}$. A sufficiently small fixed $v_1>0$ puts every internal distance in the shell. For all large $t$, distances between clusters exceed one.

All these configurations have zero core energy. Assign unordered sets of $m_0$ labels to the $k$ centers and choose one deterministic ordering of each set for the root and vertices. Different assignments give disjoint integration regions, and the relative coordinates have Jacobian one. Thus $$Z_{mP,m_0k}^{\psi_t}(\beta)
 \ge
 \left(\frac{v_0(v_1e^{-3t})^{m_0-1}}{m_0!}\right)^k .$$ First let $m\to\infty$, then $t\to\infty$. The contribution to $q_t$ has lower limit $$\frac{M_\ell}{P^3}\bigl(m_0h-3(m_0-1)\bigr),
 \qquad m_0=1,4.$$ The empty configuration supplies zero. Finally let $\ell\to\infty$ and $\eta\downarrow0$. This proves the pointwise limit. Local uniform convergence follows again from finite convex convergence on $\Omega$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The ideal transform and its maximizing densities

Let $H_t=H_{\psi_t}$ and $P_1^t=T_Aq_t$. We will need more than convergence of these functions: we must also locate all limits of their maximizing densities. For a finite convex function $F$ on an open convex set, write $\partial F(\lambda)$ for the vectors $v$ satisfying $F(y)\ge F(\lambda)+v\cdot(y-\lambda)$ throughout that set. These subgradients exist at every point, are locally bounded, and pass to limits under local uniform convergence. The last two claims follow by applying the supporting inequality to points on a fixed small ball about the argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-initial-transform"></a>
<div id="lem:initial-transform" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:ideal-three-planes"></a>
**Lemma 10**. *Uniformly on compact subsets of $\Omega$, $$\label{eq:ideal-three-planes}
 P_1^t(\beta,h)\longrightarrow
 P_*(\beta,h)
 :=\max\left(0,\ ph+\frac{\beta Ap^2}{2},\
                     4ph-9p+8\beta Ap^2\right).$$ Moreover, if $t_j\to\infty$, $(\beta_j,h_j)\to(\beta,h)\in\Omega$, and $d_j$ maximizes the expression defining $P_1^{t_j}(\beta_j,h_j)$, then the $d_j$ are bounded. Every subsequential limit belongs to $\{0,p,4p\}$ and maximizes the corresponding limiting expression.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-initial-transform"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-initial-transform</summary>

*Proof.* Applying the first inequality in <a href="#eq:superstable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:superstable">[eq:superstable]</a> to the pure core gives <a href="#eq:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coercivity">[eq:coercivity]</a> with $\kappa=t^2b^3/8$ and $B_0=t^2/2$. Consequently $$H_t(\beta,d)
 \ge \beta t\left(\frac{b^3d^2}{8}-\frac d2\right)
                       +\frac{d\log d-d}{t}.$$ For large $t$, the quadratic in this bound dominates the added term $\beta Ad^2/2$. On a fixed compact parameter set, the transformed objective is negative for $d\ge D$, with $D$ independent of all large $t$. Since $d=0$ gives zero, all maximizers lie in $[0,D]$.

The partial conjugate of $q_*$ is $$H_*(d)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&0\le d\le p,\\
  3(d-p),&p\le d\le4p,\\
  +\infty,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Consider a subsequence with $d_j\to d$. For every $z\in\mathbb R$, Fenchel’s inequality gives $$P_1^{t_j}(\beta_j,h_j)
 \le q_{t_j}(\beta_j,z)+(h_j-z)d_j
                                      +\frac{\beta_jAd_j^2}{2}.$$ Take the upper limit and then the infimum over $z$. The result is bounded by $$hd-H_*(d)+\frac{\beta Ad^2}{2}.$$ In particular $d\in[0,4p]$, since the transform is nonnegative. This objective is strictly convex on each of the two linearity intervals of $H_*$; its maximum is therefore the right side of <a href="#eq:ideal-three-planes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-three-planes">[eq:ideal-three-planes]</a>.

<a id="eq:strict-density-support"></a>
For the matching lower bound, choose a maximizing $d_0\in\{0,p,4p\}$ for this limiting objective. Its global optimality says that, for every $d'$, $$\label{eq:strict-density-support}
 H_*(d')\ge H_*(d_0)
 +(h+\beta Ad_0)(d'-d_0)
 +\frac{\beta A}{2}(d'-d_0)^2.$$ Thus $z=h+\beta Ad_0$ exposes the unique conjugate density $d_0$ for $q_*$. Set $z_j=h_j+\beta_jAd_0$, and choose a subgradient $(s'_j,d'_j)\in\partial q_{t_j}(\beta_j,z_j)$. The physical pressure is nondecreasing in $h$, so $d'_j\ge0$; these are admissible densities for the transform. By Proposition <a href="#prop:free-pressure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:free-pressure">9</a> and <a href="#eq:strict-density-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-density-support">[eq:strict-density-support]</a>, every cluster here is $(0,d_0)$. Fenchel equality at $d'_j$ therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 P_1^{t_j}(\beta_j,h_j)
 &\ge q_{t_j}(\beta_j,z_j)
       +(h_j-z_j)d'_j+\frac{\beta_jA(d'_j)^2}{2}\\
 &\longrightarrow P_*(\beta,h).
\end{aligned}$$ The upper and lower limits agree. Equality in the upper argument forces every limiting maximizing density to maximize the limiting objective, and strict convexity on its two intervals confines such densities to $\{0,p,4p\}$. The argument allows moving parameters; it proves the assertions, including local uniform convergence. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Joint supports and the initial triple

<a id="eq:reachable-gradient-formula"></a>
We next turn the maximizing densities into full supporting vectors. We first record the support identities needed for this step. For a finite convex function $F$ on $\Omega$, let $$G_F(\lambda)=
 \left\{\lim_{j\to\infty}\nabla F(\lambda_j):
 \lambda_j\to\lambda,\quad F\text{ is differentiable at }\lambda_j\right\}.$$ Local Lipschitz continuity, almost-everywhere differentiability, and the supporting inequalities give the reachable-gradient formula (Rockafellar and Wets 1998, Theorem 9.61) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[RockafellarWets1998](#ref-RockafellarWets1998)] $$\label{eq:reachable-gradient-formula}
 \partial F(\lambda)=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}G_F(\lambda),$$ where $G_F(\lambda)$ is nonempty and compact. Lemma <a href="#lem:reachable-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reachable-local">17</a> proves this identity and the supporting facts in the planar setting needed here.

For a finite concave function of $\beta>0$, write $\partial_\beta^+$ for its superdifferential. Thus $e\in-\partial_\beta^+H(\beta_0,d)$ means $$H(\beta,d)\le H(\beta_0,d)-e(\beta-\beta_0)
 \qquad(\beta>0).$$ The following identity links these canonical slopes to joint pressure supports.

<a id="stmt-lem-joint-support"></a>
<div id="lem:joint-support" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Joint Fenchel supports). *Let $Q$ be finite and convex on $\Omega$, and let $$H(\beta,d)=\sup_{h\in\mathbb R}\{hd-Q(\beta,h)\}.$$ Fix $d\ge0$ for which $H(\,\cdot\,,d)$ is finite and concave. If $(\beta_0,z)\in\Omega$ satisfies $$H(\beta_0,d)=zd-Q(\beta_0,z),$$ then <a id="eq:joint-support-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-support-identity}
 \{e\in\mathbb R:(e,d)\in\partial Q(\beta_0,z)\}
       =-\partial_\beta^+H(\beta_0,d).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-joint-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-joint-support</summary>

*Proof.* For $e$ in the right side and every $(\beta,h)\in\Omega$, the defining conjugate inequality gives $$\begin{aligned}
 Q(\beta,h)
 &\ge hd-H(\beta,d)\\
 &\ge Q(\beta_0,z)+e(\beta-\beta_0)+d(h-z).
\end{aligned}$$ This is the joint supporting inequality. Conversely, a support $(e,d)$ gives $$hd-Q(\beta,h)\le
 zd-Q(\beta_0,z)-e(\beta-\beta_0)$$ for every $h$. Take the supremum in $h$ to obtain the supergradient inequality for $H(\,\cdot\,,d)$. ◻

</details>

The next observation records how a density maximizing a quadratic transform also supports the original pressure at a shifted field. It transfers the full supporting vector, including its first coordinate, which density convergence alone would not control.

<a id="stmt-lem-transform-support"></a>
<div id="lem:transform-support" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:quadratic-density-support"></a>
<a id="eq:transform-support-transfer"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Supports under a quadratic transform). *Let $t$ satisfy <a href="#eq:t-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:t-choice">[eq:t-choice]</a>, let $Q=Q_\varphi$ for a potential in <a href="#eq:physical-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-class">[eq:physical-class]</a>, and let $0<a\le A+1$. Put $H=H_Q$ and $F=T_aQ$. Suppose that $d\ge0$ attains the supremum defining $F(\beta,h)$ at $(\beta,h)\in\Omega$, and put $z=h+\beta ad$. Then, for every $d'\ge0$, $$\label{eq:quadratic-density-support}
 H(\beta,d')\ge H(\beta,d)+z(d'-d)
                         +\frac{\beta a}{2}(d'-d)^2.$$ The finite concave function $H(\,\cdot\,,d)$ has a supergradient at $\beta$. For every $e\in-\partial_\beta^+H(\beta,d)$, $$\label{eq:transform-support-transfer}
 (e,d)\in\partial Q(\beta,z),\qquad
 \left(e+\frac{ad^2}{2},d\right)\in\partial F(\beta,h).$$ If $F$ is differentiable at $(\beta,h)$, the second vector equals $\nabla F(\beta,h)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transform-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transform-support</summary>

*Proof.* Compare the transformed objective at $d$ with its value at $d'$ and complete the square to obtain <a href="#eq:quadratic-density-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-density-support">[eq:quadratic-density-support]</a>. Dropping the nonnegative quadratic shows that $z$ supports $H(\beta,\cdot)$ at $d$, so Fenchel equality holds. Lemma <a href="#lem:joint-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:joint-support">11</a> gives the first inclusion in <a href="#eq:transform-support-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform-support-transfer">[eq:transform-support-transfer]</a>. For the second, the fixed-density branch $$(\beta',h')\longmapsto h'd-H(\beta',d)+\frac{\beta'a d^2}{2}$$ lies below $F$ and agrees with it at $(\beta,h)$. The canonical supergradient $-e$ gives this branch the displayed supporting vector, which therefore supports $F$ as well. At a differentiability point there is only one supporting vector. ◻

</details>

The three planes in <a href="#eq:ideal-three-planes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-three-planes">[eq:ideal-three-planes]</a> meet at $\lambda_*=(1,-3/4)$. Their gradients are <a id="eq:triangle-vertices"></a>$$\label{eq:triangle-vertices}
 v_1=(0,0),\qquad
 v_2=(Ap^2/2,p)=(3p/4,p),\qquad
 v_3=(8Ap^2,4p)=(12p,4p).$$ They are noncollinear, since $\det(v_2,v_3)=-9p^2\ne0$. Fix their barycenter <a id="eq:fixed-center"></a>$$\label{eq:fixed-center}
 g=\frac{v_1+v_2+v_3}{3}
   =\left(\frac{17p}{4},\frac{5p}{3}\right).$$ Choose $\tau>0$ small enough that the closed balls $$U_i=\overline B(v_i,4\tau),\qquad i=1,2,3,$$ have strictly ordered disjoint projections onto the density coordinate, with the second projection contained in $(0,\infty)$, and have the following properties. Every triangle with one vertex in each $U_i$ is noncollinear and contains a common ball $\overline B(g,\gamma)$, for some $\gamma>0$; also $g$ lies in the convex hull of no two of these balls. Such a choice follows from the strict interior position of $g$ in the original triangle and compactness. Set $$r_0=\frac14,\qquad W=\overline B(\lambda_*,r_0)\subset\Omega .$$ These constants will remain fixed throughout the construction. Figure <a href="#fig:robust-triple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:robust-triple">1</a> illustrates the common-ball property and the separated density projections.

<figure id="fig:robust-triple">

<figcaption>Schematic geometry of the three slope neighborhoods. The two triangles illustrate different choices of one point from each $U_{i}$. The choice of the neighborhoods guarantees $\overline B(g,\gamma)\subset\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{u_1,u_2,u_3\}$ for every $u_{i} ∈ U_{i}$, while $g$ belongs to the convex hull of no two neighborhoods. Their density projections $π_{2}U_{i}$, where $π_{2}(s,d) = d$, are disjoint. The actual supporting groups lie in $d ≥ 0$, although $U_{1}$ extends below that line.</figcaption>
</figure>
